Title: Finite-N BMN index across all vacuum sectors

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2605.25560v1

Published Time: Tue, 26 May 2026 01:57:06 GMT

Number of Pages: 117

# Finite-N BMN index across all vacuum sectors 

Chi-Ming Chang a,b,c , Sarthak Duary a, Kangning Liu a,d aYau Mathematical Sciences Center (YMSC), Tsinghua University, Beijing, China 

> b

Beijing Institute of Mathematical Sciences and Applications (BIMSA) Beijing, China 

> c

Peng Huanwu Center for Fundamental Theory, Hefei, Anhui, China 

> d

Department of Mathematical Sciences, Tsinghua University, Beijing, China 

cmchang@tsinghua.edu.cn, sarthakduary@tsinghua.edu.cn, lkn22@mails.tsinghua.edu.cn 

Abstract 

We compute the finite-N Witten index of BMN matrix quantum mechanics af-ter summing over all partition-labeled supersymmetric vacuum sectors. Starting from the unitary-matrix integral for each sector, we develop two complementary evaluation methods: a symmetric-group character expansion, which reduces each fixed fugacity order to a finite combinatorial sum, and a residue expansion in which the contribut-ing poles are organized by rooted trees, with a colored-tree generalization for multi-partition sectors. Direct integration and constant-term extraction provide independent checks in explicit examples. We evaluate every vacuum sector for N ≤ 9. In the equal-fugacity expansion, the coefficients near charges j ∼ N 2 show entropy growth of order 

N 2, and the sector sum does not cancel this growth. The finite-N data also reveal a nontrivial sectoral organization: near j = N 2, the sector giving the largest contribution changes with N , from single-partition sectors at small rank to double-partition sectors starting at N = 5. We call this phenomenon dominance switching. These results provide a quantitative finite-N foundation for using the BMN index as a diagnostic of protected plane-wave black-hole microstates and suggest a bulk interpretation in terms of plane-wave black holes dressed by macroscopic M2 giant gravitons, analogous to dual dressed black holes in AdS 5 × S5.

> arXiv:2605.25560v1 [hep-th] 25 May 2026

21 Introduction 

The BMN matrix quantum mechanics [1] is a massive deformation of the BFSS matrix quan-tum mechanics [2, 3] describing the DLCQ of M-theory on the maximally supersymmetric plane-wave background. The deformation lifts the flat directions, yielding a weak-coupling regime, a discrete spectrum, and a family of supersymmetric vacua described by fuzzy-sphere configurations labeled by partitions of the rank N [1,4]. Early perturbative analyses clarified the deformed symmetry structure and identified protected short multiplets in the discrete spectrum [5–7]. The BMN model also admits rich brane and holographic interpretations. In one large-N

scaling, where the multiplicities are held fixed and the sizes of the irreducible blocks are taken large, its partition-labeled fuzzy-sphere vacua describe spherical M2-branes [1]. In the complementary large-multiplicity scaling, the same partition data admit transverse M5-brane and Lin-Maldacena geometric interpretations [4,8,9]. The BMN model also sits inside a broader web of theories with SU (2 |4) symmetry, including consistent truncations related to N = 4 SYM on R×S3 [10,8,11,12]. Exact partition-function and localization methods for this family of theories were further developed in [13,14]. At strong coupling, the BMN model itself exhibits a confined phase connected to the Lin-Maldacena description and a deconfined phase described by a black-hole geometry [15]. This makes it natural to ask whether finite-N

protected sectors in the BMN model already retain signatures of black-hole microstates. Recent work on finite-N protected sectors in related holographic settings has empha-sized that black-hole microstates are not visible in naive large-N graviton counting. Explicit constructions of non-graviton operators, finite-N black-hole cohomologies, and the mono-tone/fortuitous distinction suggest that such states can instead appear as intrinsically finite-

N protected sectors [16–21]. The BMN model therefore provides a controlled and especially rich setting in which to study how the large-N behavior of protected sectors can differ sub-stantially across its many supersymmetric sectors. A recent Hamiltonian analysis derived the BMN Witten index in a weak-coupling expansion around all fuzzy-sphere vacua and found a black-hole-like growth of entropy scaling as O(N 2) at charges of order N 2 already in the trivial vacuum sector [22]. In this paper we ask how that sector-by-sector picture changes after the sum over sectors: do different vacua reinforce this growth, or can cancellations suppress the total index at charges of order N 2?More precisely, each partition 

N =

> K

X

> k=1

nkNk (1.1) with distinct positive integers Nk defines a distinct weak-coupling sector with unbroken gauge group Q 

> k

U (nk), and the full index is obtained only after summing over all such 3sectors. Determining how this sector sum reshapes the total index is necessary before the BMN index can be used as a diagnostic of plane-wave black-hole entropy, and it is also a prerequisite for isolating genuinely finite-N states of the fortuitous type discussed in [16, 18, 20, 19, 21]. Throughout this paper we work at finite mass deformation, where the weak-coupling expansion around each vacuum is under control; we do not attempt to resolve the singular BFSS limit. Our main technical goal is to make the finite-N BMN index tractable across vacuum sectors. Starting from the matrix-integral formula of [22], we develop two complementary reorganizations. First, we rewrite the gauge projection in terms of symmetric-group char-acters, following [23–25], so that at each fixed fugacity order the unitary-matrix integral becomes a finite combinatorial sum. Second, we derive a residue representation of the same index and recast the contributing residues as sums over trees. These two descriptions pro-vide independent computational handles on the same protected quantity and yield nontrivial consistency checks on the explicit series coefficients. Using these methods, we compute the index in every vacuum sector for N ≤ 9; in this range, single-, double-, and triple-partition sectors already cover all partitions of N .Specializing to equal fugacities and writing 

IBMN (t) = X

> j

dj tj , (1.2) we find that the coefficients at charges j ∼ N 2 exhibit O(N 2) growth. Within the finite-N

data computed here, we do not observe cancellations strong enough to remove this growth from the total index. We also uncover a more refined finite-N phenomenon: the sector giving the largest contribution to |dj | is not fixed once and for all, but changes with N . In particular, near j = N 2 the dominant contribution comes from single-partition sectors for small N , while double-partition sectors take over starting at N = 5. We refer to this pattern as dominance switching .This sectoral structure also has a natural bulk interpretation. If the dominant sector at large N contains an irreducible block whose size grows without bound, then the corre-sponding fuzzy sphere is a macroscopic plane-wave giant graviton, or spherical M2-brane. As argued in section 6, a simple comparison between its radius and the expected black-hole horizon scale suggests that the protected states counted by the dominant BMN index sector should be viewed not as pure eleven-dimensional supergravity black holes, but as plane-wave black holes dressed by large M2 giant gravitons. This is closely analogous in spirit to dual dressed black holes in AdS 5 × S5, where a black-hole core is dressed by large dual giant gravitons, namely D3-branes wrapping an S3 in AdS 5 [26, 27]. We also recall the related brane-fused black-hole cohomologies of [28]. The analogy is heuristic, but it gives a concrete 4way to interpret the sector dependence of the BMN index and the absence so far of a pure plane-wave supergravity black-hole solution. The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In section 2 we review the BMN Hamilto-nian and the weak-coupling expression for the index. Section 3 develops the symmetric-group character expansion. The single-partition case is treated in section 3.1, while the general multi-partition index is discussed in section 3.2. Section 4 presents the residue expansion of the BMN index. In particular, section 4.1 explains how the relevant residues can be organized as a sum over trees. Section 4.2 discusses the meaning of the contribution of the trees. Section 4.3 extends the construction to a general partition sector. Section 4.4 compares the resulting formulas in explicit multi-partition examples using direct integral, and constant term extraction. Section 5 uses the computed series to analyze the growth of log |dj | and the onset of dominance switching. Section 6 discusses the main conclusions. Appendix A sets up the partition notation and records a basic exponential identity used in section 3. Appendix B collects the corresponding SU (N ) BMN indices. The single-partition, double-partition, triple-partition, and total-index cases are presented separately in Appendices B.1, B.2, B.3, and B.4, respectively. Appendix C derives closed-form expres-sions for the single-partition SU (N ) BMN index. More specifically, Appendix C.1 focuses on the trivial vacuum and presents the closed-form SU (N ) BMN indices for N = 2 , . . . , 7, obtained by evaluating the residues as a sum over trees as described in section 4.1. Ap-pendix C.2 then treats the remaining single-partition sectors. Appendix D develops an iter-ative residue-evaluation procedure for the single-partition BMN index. Finally, Appendix E gives a detailed illustration of the character expansion method by working out the explicit sector N1 = 1 , N 2 = 2 , n 1 = 1 , n 2 = 1. 

# 2 Preliminaries of BMN matrix quantum mechanics 

In section 2, we briefly review the Hamiltonian of BMN matrix quantum mechanics, and summarize the Witten index computation using Hamiltonian formulation from [22]. 

## 2.1 The BMN Hamiltonian 

The total Hamiltonian for the BMN matrix quantum mechanics can be decomposed into two components, H0 and Hint , expressed as [4] 

H = H0 + Hint . (2.1) Here, H0 corresponds to the BFSS matrix quantum mechanics limit, while Hint contains the mass and deformation terms dependent on the parameter μ. Explicitly, these terms are given 5by

The terms in Hint break this symmetry, reducing it to SO (3) × SO (6). 

## 2.2 Witten index review 

The BMN matrix quantum mechanics is characterized by SU (2 |4) supersymmetry. The maximal bosonic subgroup of this symmetry is SO (3) ×SO (6). The supersymmetry includes sixteen supercharges, denoted as Qmα and their Hermitian conjugates ( Qmα )†, where α = ±

represents the spinor index associated with SU (2) ∼= SO (3). These supercharges obey the following anti-commutation relations [5] 

{Qmα , (Qnβ )†} = 2 δmn δαβ H + μ

. This structure ensures that bosonic and fermionic states contribute with opposite signs to the trace. States with ∆ > 0 form supersymmetric doublets ( |Ψ⟩ and Q|Ψ⟩) whose contributions cancel exactly. Therefore, the Witten index receives contributions solely from BPS states (where ∆ = 0). As a result, the Witten index is independent of the inverse temperature β. The Witten index remains invariant under variations of the coupling constant g, except potentially at the singular limit of infinite coupling ( g → ∞ ).

In this section, we recast the BMN index in terms of symmetric-group characters, following [23–25]. The central ingredient is the Frobenius formula, which expresses multitraces of unitary matrices in terms of products of unitary-group characters and symmetric-group characters. The Haar integral in (2.13) is then evaluated using character orthogonality. We shall refer to this reorganization as the symmetric-group character expansion . We stress that this does not alter the index; it is merely a different, more algebraic way of presenting the same quantity. Its main advantage is computational: the original gauge projection, written as a unitary-matrix integral in (2.13), is transformed into a finite combinatorial problem involving integer partitions and symmetric-group characters. To keep the notation light, we specialize to equal fugacities ∆1 = ∆ 2 = ∆ 3 = ∆ , t2 = e−∆. (3.1) The single-letter indices (2.12) simplify to

# 4 Residue expansion of the BMN index 

In this section, we develop an alternative approach to computing the index based on the residue theorem. The unitary-matrix integral (2.13) over the matrices Uk can first be reduced to an integral over their eigenvalues yk,a , a = 1 , · · · , n k, as

All products over multisets are understood with multiplicity. The contour integral can then be evaluated by the residue theorem, which converts the integral into a sum over residues. One subtlety of this method is the organization of the poles. Although the final result is independent of the order of integration, the set of poles that appears at intermediate steps does depend on that order. We first explain the combinatorics in the single-partition sector. In this case the pairwise data are independent of I, J , and the index takes the form

The extra Vandermonde zero precisely compensates the difference |P | − | Z| = 1, so zi = 0 is not a pole of the shifted integrand. We organize the residue theorem as a sum of simple-pole data. In particular, we do not introduce separate subscripted variables such as fij . Instead, we sum directly over rooted trees and separate colliding poles by a small ϵ-deformation. The admissible trees are characterized by the following rules. 1. A root edge 

u → i

means that the zi-contour is evaluated at the pole zi = u coming from ( zi − u)−1.2. A non-root edge 

i f

−→ j, f ∈ P, 

means that the residue of the zj -integral is taken at the pole zj = f z i.3. Each physical vertex has one and only one incoming edge. This is the simple-pole prescription. 4. The root has exactly one outgoing edge. Indeed, if both u → i and u → j with i̸ = j

were present, then zi = zj = u, and the Vandermonde factor

−→ j encodes the pole zj = f z i.Thus every non-vanishing contribution is represented by a rooted tree. Once a canonical traversal order is fixed, each unlabeled tree represents N ! equivalent labelings of the eigenval-ues, and this cancels the prefactor 1 /N ! in the integral. Using this canonical ordering, one can generate the trees recursively by adding a largest node to a tree. From now on, we will always use this canonical lexicographical order. Equivalent classes 

For practical evaluation it is useful to group trees before taking the final fugacity limits. In the trivial vacuum sector, two trees are called t-equivalent if their undeformed evaluations 

{z∗ 

> i

} become equal, up to permutation, after the specialization 

gi → t4, f1,2,3 → t2, f4 → t6.

18 They are called BMN-equivalent if the same happens, again up to permutation, after impos-ing the BMN relations 

g1 → f2f3, g2 → f3f1, g3 → f1f2, f4 → f1f2f3.

Each t-equivalence class is a union of BMN-equivalence classes. In practice, the ϵ-limit inside a BMN-equivalence class is usually mild; the more expensive step is the final t-expansion, where it is advantageous to combine terms with the same denominator structure before expanding. 

## 4.2 Meaning of the trees 

We now explain the geometric meaning of the residue trees. The main point is that a tree is not a new physical object by itself. Rather, it is a prescription for a residue channel: it tells us which denominator factors are used to localize the eigenvalue variables. For simplicity, first consider the single-partition case. A tree has one root edge 

u → r, 

which selects the pole 

zr = u, 

and every non-root edge 

i f

−→ j

selects the pole 

zj = f z i.

Therefore the tree fixes all eigenvalues recursively: 

z∗ 

> j

= u Y

> e∈Path Γ(u,j )

f e.

Equivalently, the tree selects n denominator hypersurfaces whose intersection is the pole 

pΓ = ( z∗ 

> 1

, . . . , z ∗

> n

). When this pole is isolated in the full divisor arrangement, the picture is simple. Around each selected denominator one draws a small circle in the normal direction. The product of these circles is a small n-torus, 

TΓ : |h1| = δ1, . . . , |hn| = δn,

19 and the tree contribution is the corresponding period of the meromorphic n-form, Cont(Γ) = 1(2 πi )n

Z

> TΓ

ω. 

In this clean situation, the tree is literally represented by a small residue torus. The subtlety is that the BMN integrand contains many denominator factors, not only the n factors selected by a given tree. Thus several trees may land on the same undeformed pole. At such a collision point, the individual small tori attached to the different trees are no longer separated inside the complement of the full divisor. They should not be regarded as independent contours of the original undeformed integral. It is useful to phrase this in terms of a local moduli space of hyperplane arrangements. Let 

Dλ = [

> A

HA(λ)be the union of all denominator hypersurfaces, where λ denotes the parameters controlling their relative positions. In our application, λ is represented by the small ϵ-deformation. We write 

Mλ = Cn \ Dλ.

For generic λ, the relevant intersections are separated, and each tree gives a genuine cycle [TΓ(λ)] ∈ Hn(Mλ).

At special values of λ, several intersections collide. Then some of these n-cycles can merge or disappear. In this sense, the top homology seen by the residue tori is a function on the arrangement moduli space: 

λ 7 −→ Hn(Mλ),

or more concretely 

λ 7 −→ bn(Mλ) := dim Hn(Mλ).

This function is locally constant on the smooth strata of the moduli space, but it can jump on the discriminant locus where hyperplanes collide. The lower-dimensional linking data are more robust. The one-cycle linking a single divisor component is still well-defined locally. What fails is the ability to combine n such one-cycles into a canonical product torus associated with one individual tree. Thus the collision is not a problem with the existence of elementary divisor links; it is a problem with separating the higher-dimensional residue cycles. A two-dimensional local model makes the picture transparent. Consider three lines through the same point, 

H1 = {x = 0 }, H2 = {y = 0 }, H = {x − y = 0 }.

20 If we try to assign a small torus to the pair ( H1, H 2), the natural candidate is 

T12 (a, b ) = {| x| = a, |y| = b}.

This contour is allowed only when it does not hit the third line H. Using 

s = y, t = xy ,

we get 

T12 (a, b ) : |s| = b, |t| = ab .

The third line is simply 

t = 1 .

Thus the ratio a/b matters. If a < b , the circle |t| = a/b lies inside t = 1. If a > b , it lies outside t = 1. To move from one choice to the other, the contour must cross the wall 

a = b, 

where it hits the extra divisor H. Therefore, in the undeformed central arrangement, there is no canonical small torus attached only to the pair ( H1, H 2). The extra line passing through the same point obstructs the separation of residue channels. This can also be seen directly by computing the homology group: 

H2(C − H1 − H2 − H) ∼= Z2 (4.16) Now deform the third line slightly: 

eH = {x − y = ϵ}, ϵ̸ = 0 .

The three pairwise intersections are separated: 

p12 = (0 , 0) , p1 eH = (0 , −ϵ), p2 eH = ( ϵ, 0) .

Near each point, there is now an honest small residue torus linking only the two relevant lines. Indeed, we have 

H2(C − H1 − H2 − eH) ∼= Z3 (4.17) Thus, the deformation resolves one degenerate pole into several separated simple residue channels. This is exactly the role of the ϵ-deformation in the tree formula. At finite ϵ, the collid-ing trees move to nearby but distinct points in the arrangement moduli space. Each tree then has a well-defined local residue contour. In the limit ϵ → 0, these contours degenerate 21 and the individual tree contributions may become singular. However, the periods are mero-morphic functions of the deformation parameter, and the original BMN contour integral is nonsingular. Therefore the singularities cancel only after summing over all trees in the same collision class: lim 

> ϵ→0

X

> Γ in a collision class

Cont ϵ(Γ) .

The individual terms are regulator-dependent residue channels; the sum is the intrinsic contribution of the undeformed pole. This gives the physical interpretation of the trees. A non-colliding tree is a genuine small residue torus. A colliding tree is one branch of a resolved degenerate residue. The 

ϵ-deformation should therefore be viewed as a contour regulator: it moves the hyperplane arrangement away from the discriminant locus, computes the separated periods, and then takes the coincident limit after summing over the colliding branches. 

## 4.3 Generalization to a general partition sector 

We now return to the full BMN sector {nk; Nk}Kk=1 . Since the pairwise notation has already been introduced in (4.2), only the tree rules need to be generalized. A physical vertex is I = ( k, a ) with color c(I) = k. A non-root edge 

I f

−→ J

is allowed only if f ∈ PIJ , in which case it imposes 

yJ = f y I .

A root edge u → J corresponds to the pole yJ = u. The admissible colored trees satisfy the following rules. 1. Each physical vertex has one and only one incoming edge. 2. The root may have several outgoing edges, but at most one to each color. If u → J

and u → J′ with c(J) = c(J′) and J̸ = J′, then yJ = yJ′ = u, and the same-color Vandermonde zero kills the residue. 3. More generally, fix a parent vertex I, a target color r, and a label f . Then there is at most one outgoing edge 

I f

−→ J with c(J) = r. 

If two such edges existed, the two target vertices would coincide, and the same-color Vandermonde factor would again force the residue to vanish. 22 4. Before adjoining the auxiliary root u, the diagram is therefore a colored forest; after adjoining u, it becomes a rooted colored tree. 5. After fixing a canonical order within each color class, each unlabeled colored tree represents Q

In order to test the validity of the formula (4.18), we compute the following two cases using the tree method in double partition sectors: ( N1, N 2, n 1, n 2) = (1 , 2, 1, 1) and ( N1, N 2, n 1, n 2) = (1 , 2, 2, 1). For, the case ( N1, N 2, n 1, n 2) = (1 , 2, 2, 1), the tree method agrees with the character table expansion in (B.42). 24 4.4 Computation of the BMN index for multi-partition sectors: consistency check 

In this section, we will compute the BMN index for multi-partition sectors using direct integral methods. These results match the symmetric-group character expansion results of the BMN indices for the same sectors, providing a non-trivial consistency check of our results. We express the double and triple partition sector indices in Appendices B.2 and B.3 using equations (3.24) and (3.25). To illustrate the character expansion approach, Appendix E provides a detailed walkthrough of the computation for a particular example N1 = 1 , N 2 =2, n 1 = 1 , n 2 = 1.

To optimize the double-partition integral, we introduce the relative fugacity projection, exploiting the fact that the integrand depends strictly on the phase difference. Because the integrand of the BMN index depends exclusively on the relative phase difference. We exploit this by introducing a relative fugacity, x = ei(ϕ1−ϕ2). This allows us to map the continuous Haar measure integrals to a purely algebraic constant term extraction acting on the Taylor-expanded plethystic exponential. 

## Relative fugacity projection approach N = N1 + N2

The exact integration of the double-partition BMN index can be vastly optimized by transi-tioning to a fugacity basis and employing constant-term extraction. Because the integrand depends exclusively on the phase difference ϕ2 − ϕ1, the overall U (1) gauge phase decouples, allowing us to reduce the two-dimensional integral to a single projection. We define a rel-ative fugacity x = ei(ϕ1−ϕ2). The cosine term naturally reorganizes into pairs of reciprocal variables 2 cos  m(ϕ2 − ϕ1) = xm + x−m. ## N = n1N1 + n2N2

The fundamental bottleneck in evaluating the multi-partition BMN index directly from its matrix integral definition is the presence of continuous multidimensional integration over the gauge group Haar measures. We implemented an algorithmic strategy in Mathematica in using constant term extraction that maps the exact integrals into discrete, truncated polynomial algebra.

All results match with the results in B.2 using character expansion approach. To compute the general multi-partition BMN index, first, we express the system using relative fugacities for all gauge nodes, fixing one variable to remove redundant overall phase 31 symmetry. We then construct a finite Laurent polynomial by expanding the Plethystic expo-nential of the single-letter index up to the desired order in t, multiplied by the Vandermonde determinants from the Haar measures. Then, we simply extract the constant term (the co-efficient where all fugacity powers are zero) from this polynomial. Finally, we divide by the universal U (1) factor to isolate the physical SU (N ) index. This method transforms a difficult multidimensional integration problem into a fast, exact polynomial coefficient extraction. 

# 5 Analysis of the finite-N BMN index data 

In this section, we analyze the finite-N data extracted from the BMN index in the single-, double-, triple-partition, and total sectors. Our goal is to quantify the growth of the coefficients dj at charges of order j ∼ N 2 and to compare how this growth depends on the vacuum sector. To this end, we study the coefficient at j = N 2, plot log |dj=N 2 | as a function of N 2, and perform linear fits to test the O(N 2) scaling. We also examine log |dj=N 2 |/N 2 as a function of N . This provides a quantitative summary of the finite-N behavior of the BMN index and clarifies which sectors dominate the growth in the range of data available here. Fitting log |dj | with N 2 for single partition index, double partition index, triple partition index and total index we get Fig. .2. We can now compare the total entropy across different partition sectors by first adding up all indices belonging to each sector (single, double, triple) and then identifying which sector produces the largest combined result. For small N (N = 2 , 3, 4), the dominant contribution in entropy at the sector-summed level comes from the single-partition sector. Starting at 

N = 5, the double-partition sector takes over and becomes dominant, as shown in Table 7. Moreover, the data suggest that this trend continues as N increases. In particular, by N = 9 the dominant double-partition entropy, 22 .9678, and the dominant triple-partition entropy, 22 .8191, are already extremely close, with a difference of only 0 .1487. It is therefore natural to expect that, for larger N , sectors with more partitions — triple, quadruple, and so on — will successively overtake the lower-partition sectors and become dominant in entropy. The data in Table 6 show that the entropic dominance switching persists at the sector-summed level : once N ≥ 5, the total contribution obtained by summing over all double-partition sectors exceeds the corresponding summed single-partition contribution, and for the values currently accessible also exceeds the summed triple-partition contribution. Thus the entropic dominance of the double-partition sector for N = 5 , . . . , 9 appears in two nested senses : first, a particular double-partition sector dominates at the level of individual indices, and second, the sum over the entire double-partition sector is itself dominant among the sector-summed contributions. 36 N Single log |dj | Double log |dj | Triple log |dj | Total log |dj |

# 6 Conclusion 

We computed the finite-N BMN Witten index as a fugacity expansion, including the contri-butions from all partition-labeled supersymmetric vacua. In the trivial vacuum sector, the coefficients at charges j ∼ O (N 2) already exhibit O(N 2) entropy growth [22], suggesting a BPS black-hole sector in M-theory on the plane wave. The main question addressed here was whether the full index, after summing over all vacuum sectors, is reduced by cancellations. Our explicit computations up to N = 9 show no such suppression; rather, the sector sum enhances the total index. The finite-N data also reveal a nontrivial sectoral organization. At the coefficient of 

tN 2

, the dominant sector is not fixed once and for all: different partition sectors dominate over different finite ranges, and the dominant sector jumps as the rank increases. We call this phenomenon dominance switching . It indicates that the finite-N index is not governed smoothly by a single vacuum sector, even though the entropy growth itself is robust under the sum over sectors. The main lesson for large N is therefore that the dominant sector need not be the trivial vacuum sector. We characterize the possible large-N behavior of the dominant vacuum sector by the size of its largest irreducible block, 

Nmax = max  

> i

Ni. (6.1) The key question suggested by the index is whether Nmax in the dominant sector remains order one or instead grows without bound as N → ∞ .For a BMN vacuum N = P 

> i

niNi, the largest fuzzy sphere is controlled by Nmax rather than by the multiplicities ni. These fuzzy spheres are the plane-wave giant gravitons, equiv-37 alently spherical M2-branes, of [1]. Using the standard BMN normalization and the eleven-dimensional length conversion, the physical size of the largest such brane scales, up to nu-merical constants, as 

rfuzzy 

ℓP

∼ N 1/3λ−1/3μ N max . (6.2) On the gravity side, the strong-coupling black-hole scale of [15] gives the expected horizon scaling 2

rhorizon 

ℓP

∼ N 1/3μ2/5λ−2/15 . (6.3) Combining the two scales and using the dimensionless BMN coupling g = λ/μ 3 gives 

rfuzzy 

rhorizon 

∼ Nmax g−1/5. (6.4) Thus any large-N dominant sector satisfying Nmax ≫ g1/5 has a fuzzy sphere parametri-cally outside the expected horizon. This is the condition actually required for the separation of scales. In the usual BMN ’t Hooft limit, where N → ∞ with fixed g, it is automatically satisfied if the dominant sector has Nmax → ∞ . More generally, in a scaling with N ≫ g,the same conclusion follows for dominant sectors whose largest block obeys Nmax ≫ g1/5,equivalently g = o(N 5max ); this extra condition is needed because Nmax can be much smaller than the total rank N . If the dominant BMN index sector indeed satisfies this separation condition, its bulk dual should not be captured by a pure horizon-like eleven-dimensional su-pergravity black hole. Rather, the leading protected states should retain macroscopic BMN giant-graviton, or spherical M2-brane, degrees of freedom outside the horizon. Equivalently, they may be viewed as a black-hole core dressed by large M2-brane degrees of freedom. This dressed-core picture is structurally similar to the recently proposed dual dressed black holes in AdS 5 ×S5, where a core black hole is surrounded by large dual giant gravitons, namely D3-branes wrapping an S3 in the AdS 5 factor [26,27]; related brane-fused black-hole cohomologies were constructed in [28]. The analogy is not a literal identification: the BMN dressing branes are plane-wave M2 giant gravitons, whereas the DDBH dressing branes are AdS dual giant gravitons. Nevertheless, it offers a possible explanation for why no pure eleven-dimensional supergravity black-hole solution asymptotic to the plane wave is currently known. The resulting interpretation is heuristic: the present evidence is finite-N , and the gravity radius used above is an expected strong-coupling scale rather than a direct BPS solution.      

> 2The black-hole solutions analyzed in [15] are thermal and non-BPS. We use only their parametric horizon-size scaling and assume that the same dependence on N,λ, and μapplies to a putative BPS black-hole branch.

38 A natural next step is to push the multi-partition computation beyond N = 9 using the three tools developed here: the symmetric-group character expansion, the residue-over-trees formula, and constant-term extraction. A larger dataset would test whether dominance switching settles into an asymptotic pattern and whether Nmax in the dominant sector grows without bound. 

Further directions. Two complementary directions are worth emphasizing. First, a re-lated but logically distinct problem is to isolate the genuinely finite-N part of the BMN protected spectrum. In finite-N N = 4 SYM, BPS operators can be separated into mono-tone operators, which remain BPS for all N , and fortuitous operators, which become BPS only at special finite values of N [20]. Fortuity has been explored in several related sys-tems [16–18, 31, 32, 19, 20, 33, 21, 34–41]. In the BMN model, one would like to define an analogous black-hole index, at least at the level of protected indices, by subtracting the monotone, graviton-like contribution from the full finite-N protected index. The sector-summed Witten index computed here is a necessary input for such a subtraction, but the subtraction itself would require an independent characterization of the BMN monotone con-tribution. Carrying this out would give a sharper way to locate BMN fortuitous sectors before attempting the explicit construction of the corresponding operators. Second, it would be useful to derive the BMN index directly by supersymmetric localization. A natural start-ing point is the four-dimensional construction of the N = 4 SYM index on S1 × S3, where the index is realized as a Scherk–Schwarz-deformed path integral and computed by localiza-tion [42]. Since BMN matrix quantum mechanics arises from N = 4 SYM on R × S3 by the consistent truncation to the lowest Kaluza–Klein modes on S3 [10, section 2], reducing this localization construction on S3 may lead to a localization derivation of the BMN index itself.

# D Single partition BMN index using iterative residue evaluation 

In this Appendix D, we evaluate the single partition BMN index directly by iterative contour integration. The method is conceptually straightforward: one performs the contour integrals successively, identifying the poles at each stage and summing their residues. Its limitation is also clear: the pole structure is generated dynamically, so after each integration new singularities appear in the remaining variables. Consequently, while the method is effective for low-rank examples, it becomes increasingly inefficient for larger n1, which motivates the residue-over-trees reorganization used in the main text section 4.1. Evaluating iterative contour integrals becomes computationally prohibitive for a sin-gle partition index N = n1N1 when n1 > 2. The primary bottleneck lies in the dynamic generation of singularities. It is difficult to identify and classify all relevant poles a priori. As each successive integration is evaluated, the resulting expression gives entirely new poles. Attempting to systematically track, organize, and compute residues in this step-by-step iter-ative manner leads to massive computational inefficiency. To circumvent this combinatorial explosion, we utilize the sum over trees formula for calculating residues. 107 BMN index in the irreducible vacuum sector

X

> λ1⊢| P11 +P12 |
> ℓ(λ1)≤1

χλ1  P11 + P12 

 χλ1  P11 + P21 

 , (E.4) and an analogous one for λ2. Here P11 + P12 denotes the partition obtained by combining the parts of P11 and P12 and similarly for P11 + P21 . The consistency condition |P12 | = |P21 |

guarantees that 

|P11 + P12 | = |P11 + P21 | and |P22 + P12 | = |P22 + P21 |, (E.5) so both characters in each product are evaluated on partitions of the same integer d. The condition ℓ(λ) ≤ 1 means that λ has at most one part. For a partition of a positive integer 

d, the only possibilities are the single-row partition λ = ( d). The empty partition (which corresponds to d = 0) is also allowed, and by convention χ∅(∅) = 1. For d > 0, the irreducible representation of the symmetric group Sd labelled by ( d) is the trivial representation . The character of the trivial representation of any finite group is 1 on every conjugacy class. For 

Sd, the conjugacy classes are indexed by partitions of d. Hence for any partition P of d,

χ(d)(P ) = 1 . (E.6) Because the sum over λ1 runs only over partitions of d1 = |P11 + P12 | with length ≤ 1, the only contribution is from λ1 = ( d1) (or the empty partition when d1 = 0). Therefore 

X

> λ1⊢d1
> ℓ(λ1)≤1

χλ1 (P11 + P12 ) χλ1 (P11 + P21 ) = χ(d1)(P11 + P12 ) χ(d1)(P11 + P21 ) = 1 · 1 = 1 . (E.7) The same reasoning applies to the λ2 sum. Hence both character sums equal 1, regardless of the specific partitions P11 , P 12 , P 21 , P 22 .We have
